Compare the coding strategies used by taste and olfaction instead of treating all chemical senses alike."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m6-3-study-1","text":"Taste as chemical detection"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Taste begins when dissolved substances interact with receptor cells in taste buds. The system contributes to decisions about food and potentially harmful substances. Different receptor mechanisms respond to broad taste qualities, such as sweet, sour, salty, bitter, and umami. The same taste quality can involve more than one molecular mechanism, and the perceived flavor of food includes smell and other sensations. A food experience cannot be explained by tongue receptors alone."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m6-3-study-2","text":"Coding and individual variation"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Taste information moves through cranial nerves and central pathways, and neural activity represents both stimulus quality and intensity. People can differ substantially in sensitivity to particular bitter compounds or other taste stimuli because of biological variation and experience. Preferences are also affected by learning and bodily state. A sensitivity difference is not the same thing as a difference in preference: someone may detect a taste accurately yet dislike it."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m6-3-study-3","text":"Olfaction and its special route"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Odor molecules bind to receptors in olfactory tissue, activating populations of neurons whose combined activity distinguishes many odorants. Signals travel to the olfactory bulb and onward to areas involved in perception, memory, and emotion. The olfactory system is organized differently from a simple one-receptor-for-one-smell model. It is especially easy to notice the integration of senses when a blocked nose changes the flavor of food."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m6-3-study-4","text":"Synesthesia and integrated perception"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Synesthesia refers to experiences in which stimulation in one domain reliably evokes an additional experience in another, such as particular colors associated with letters. It illustrates how perceptual systems can interact and how people's subjective worlds vary. It should not be confused with the normal contribution of smell to flavor. Across this module, distinguish the chemicals that initiate a signal from the neural codes and experiences that Taste and smell analyze chemicals, while hearing and touch analyze other kinds of physical change. The important organizing question is how specific receptors and neural pathways transform their distinctive stimulus energy into meaningful experience.","paragraphIndex":801611,"kind":"individually_written_classroom_explanation","level":"contextual_clarification","sourceHeadingId":"bp14-m6-3-h12","sourcePrintedPage":221,"sourcePdfPage":239,"editorialStatus":"individual_topic_specific_explanation_drafted_from_source_topic_map","evidenceBasis":"Supplied Kalat 14e PDF and matching Markdown; existing source-extracted paragraphs kept as separate verifiable references","needsIndependentReview":true}}],"classroomParagraphs":["Vision, hearing, and touch provide useful information for many purposes, but taste is useful only for telling us whether to swallow something or spit it out.","That function is more important for some species than for others.","Because they eat only fish, and swallow them whole, they have little need for the sense of taste.","Taste results from stimulation of the taste buds, the receptors on the tongue. When we talk about the taste of food, we generally mean flavor, which combines taste and smell.","Taste and smell axons converge onto many of the same cortical cells, enabling them to combine their influences.","The receptors for taste are not true neurons but modified skin cells.","Like neurons, taste receptors have excitable membranes and release neurotransmitters to excite neighboring neurons, which in turn transmit information to the brain.","Like skin cells, however, taste receptors are gradually sloughed off and replaced, each one lasting 10 to 14 days.","Mammalian taste receptors are in taste buds located in papillae on the surface of the tongue (Figure 6.19). A given papilla may contain up to 10 or more taste buds, and each taste bud contains about 50 receptor cells.","In adult humans, taste buds lie mainly along the edge of the tongue.","Then try it again on the edge of your tongue and notice the taste. It will seem as if you are moving the taste to the center of your tongue.","You attribute the taste you had on the side of your tongue to any other spot you stroke.","Traditionally, people in Western society have described tastes in terms of sweet, sour, salty, and bitter.","One way to identify taste receptor types is to find procedures that alter one receptor but not others.","For example, chewing a miracle berry (native to West Africa) gives little taste itself but temporarily changes sweet receptors. Miracle berries contain a protein—miraculin—that modifies sweet receptors, enabling acids to stimulate them.","(available via the Internet), anything acidic will taste sweet in addition to its usual sour taste for the next half hour.","Most toothpastes contain sodium lauryl sulfate, a chemical that intensifies bitter tastes while it coats the sweet receptors and prevents anything from reaching them. This demonstration is probably risky for anyone with diabetes, because G. sylvestre also alters sugar absorption in the intestines.","The overall point of these demonstrations is that we do have receptors that are sensitive to one taste or another. This phenomenon, called adaptation, reflects the fatigue of receptors sensitive to sour tastes.","Although we have long known that people have at least four kinds of taste receptors, several types of evidence suggest a fifth, glutamate, as in monosodium glutamate (MSG).","Water replaces the bicarbonate ions usually present in the saliva on the tongue, and when it does so it changes the acid-to-base ratio and mildly stimulates the same receptors that detect sour tastes. Researchers stimulated rats’ brain cells responsive to taste with an electrical pattern matching that for quinine.","Recall that a neuron produces an action potential when sodium ions cross its membrane.","A saltiness receptor simply permits sodium ions on the tongue to cross its membrane, and thereby detects the presence of sodium.","After a molecule binds to one of these receptors, it activates a G protein that releases a second messenger within the cell. People have two types of sweetness receptors and two types of umami receptors, each with somewhat different sensitivities.","Bitter taste used to be a puzzle because bitter substances include a long list of dissimilar chemicals.","The answer is that we have not one bitter receptor but a family of approximately 30 that respond to different sets of compounds.","Our large number of bitter receptors enable us to detect a great variety of dangerous chemicals. Bitter chemicals are toxic, and the body does what it can to expel them.","Let’s contrast two ways of coding sensory information.","Anything that excites a pain axon reaches the brain as a pain sensation.","In contrast, color vision requires an across-fiber pattern, or","The only way to resolve the ambiguity is to compare it with the responses of the other types of cones. Most taste receptors respond only to one type of chemical, but some respond to both sweet and salty, or sweet and umami.","The receptors feed into neurons that might respond just to one taste, or to several.","By the time information reaches cells in the insula in the cortex (Figure 6.21), most cells respond most strongly to one taste, partly to others also, and in many cases also to odors, hunger, emotions, and other influences. The insula is not arranged in clusters of cells that respond mainly to one taste or another.","Because the response of any cortical taste cell is ambiguous, the meaning requires a comparison across cells.","And if we ask people to describe how strong various foods taste, they agree on which foods have bland, strong, or very strong flavors.","However, when you say some food has a strong taste, you mean that it is strong compared to your other experiences.","Some people have three times as many taste buds as other people do on the fungiform papillae near the tip of the tongue (Figure 6.22).","Those with the greatest number are called supertasters, and those with average or below-average numbers are tasters or non-tasters (a misleading term). Several genes have significant effects, but the variation also depends on age, hormones, and other influences.","That tendency is probably adaptive, because a pregnant person needs to be careful to avoid harmful foods.","People with the most taste buds tend to dislike strongly flavored foods, especially foods with a bitter taste.","However, these tendencies have many exceptions, because food preferences depend on familiarity and other influences. For example, non-tasters are common in India, where the food is spicy, and also in Britain, with relatively bland foods.","The smell of a sweaty female increases a male’s testosterone secretions, especially if the female is near her time of ovulation.","Olfaction is the sense of smell. It begins when airborne chemicals contact receptor membranes within the nose, and it contributes strongly to food flavor as well as to detecting biologically important cues.","Kalat discusses smell as both a sensory and behavioral system: olfactory information can influence food selection, avoidance reactions, and social responses.","Chemicals reaching the nasal receptor surfaces activate olfactory receptors. Different combinations of receptor activity help distinguish among odors.","Blocking the nose during eating reduces the flavor of many foods, showing how smell contributes to what we commonly call taste.","In some animals, predator odors trigger rapid avoidance. Species vary in the specific receptors and reactions involved.","The neurons responsible for smell are the olfactory cells that line the olfactory epithelium in the rear of the nasal air","In mammals, receptors are on the olfactory cell’s cilia (threadlike dendrites) that extend into the mucous surface of the nasal passage.","Humans have several hundred types of olfactory receptors, while rats and mice have about a thousand and elephants have about two thousand. Like metabotropic neurotransmitter receptors, each olfactory receptor protein traverses the cell membrane seven times and responds to a chemical outside the cell (an odorant molecule) by triggering changes in a G protein inside the cell.","Olfactory receptors are vulnerable to damage because they are exposed to the air.","Each olfactory neuron axon contains copies of its olfactory receptor protein, which it uses like an identification card to find its correct partner.","However, if the entire olfactory surface is damaged at once by a blast of toxic fumes so that the system has to replace all the receptors at the same time, many of them fail to make the correct connections, and olfactory experience does not fully recover.","The diversity of olfactory receptors makes possible narrow specialization of functions.","Each olfactory cell has only one receptor type, and most receptors respond to either just one chemical or several closely related chemicals.","Some receptors respond synergistically; that is, they respond to a combination of two chemicals, but hardly at all to either one alone. Although the receptors are arranged haphazardly along the nasal cavity, all the receptors sensitive to a particular chemical or combination of chemicals connect to a single cluster in the olfactory bulb, called a glomerulus (Figures 3.12 and 6.25).","When we get to the primary olfactory cortex, known as the piriform cortex, things are different.","Whereas each glomerulus responds to just one chemical, each cell in the piriform cortex responds to a combination of chemicals, such as the smell of berries or melons. Because each neuron responds to a combination of influences, its response does not identify a single odorant.","Most of the genes controlling olfactory receptors have variant forms, and on average, two people chosen at random probably differ in about 30 percent of their olfactory receptor genes.","Sensitivity to mushroom odor remains nearly constant as people age, sensitivity to onion odor declines moderately, and sensitivity to rose odor declines greatly.","A sharp decline in odor sensitivity is often an early symptom of Alzheimer’s disease or Parkinson’s disease. Many people who were infected by the worldwide pandemic of COVID-19 suffered an impaired sense of smell, with slow recovery over weeks.","The virus probably does not enter human neurons, but it damages the skin cells that support the olfactory receptors in the nose.","Humane females detect odors more readily than males, at all ages and in all cultures that researchers have tested Males, prepubescent females, and post-menopausal females do not demonstrate that effect, so it apparently depends on female hormones.","Finally, let’s consider something that is not one sense but a combination: Synesthesia is the experience some people have in which stimulation of one sense evokes a perception of that sense and another one also.","People reporting synesthesia have increased amounts of gray matter in certain brain areas and altered connections to other areas.","One man with synesthesia could find the 2 consistently faster than other people, explaining that he just looked for a patch of orange! However, he was slower than other people to find an 8 among 6s because both 8 and 6 look blue to him.","Another person had trouble finding an A among 4s because both look red but could easily find an A among 0s because 0 looks black.","Researchers found 10 people with synesthesia whose associations matched or nearly matched the colors of Fisher-Price refrigerator magnets they had used as children, such as red A, yellow C, and green D.","Only a small percentage of children who play with these magnets develop synesthesia, and most people with synesthesia have other associations, so the toys represent only one part of the explanation. However, during that period, the somatosensory area of her right cortex was receiving little input.","If nonhumans could talk, most species would start by describing what they smell.","A human, a dog, a bat, and a snail may be in the same place, but the environments they perceive are very different.","People who lose their sense of taste say they no longer enjoy eating and find it difficult to swallow. Taste and smell cannot compete with vision and hearing for telling us about what is happening in the distance, but they are essential for the enjoyment of life.","They’re also presented in alphabetical order with definitions in the book’s Subject Index/Glossary at the end of this book.","The book discusses evidence that people can identify some attributes and emotional states through odor, although these effects have limits and differ among individuals."],"classroomResearch":{"title":"Evidence in context: Synesthesia","body":"Researchers found 10 people with synesthesia whose Use the book’s categories to distinguish voluntary, involuntary, and feedback-sensitive movements."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-1-study-1","text":"Muscles generate force, circuits coordinate it"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Movement depends on contractions of muscle fibers governed by motor neurons. Muscle fibers can differ in speed and endurance, making some more suited to rapid forceful actions and others to sustained activity. The nervous system must regulate both which muscles contract and how their activity is timed. Movements are not simply a sequence of commands from a single central controller; sensory feedback and distributed circuits continuously influence them."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-1-study-2","text":"Feedback and proprioception"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Proprioceptors supply information about muscle stretch, tension, and body position. Reflex pathways can respond to changes before conscious awareness, helping stabilize posture and adjust ongoing movement. Feedback is especially useful when a task is slow enough for incoming information to guide corrections. Fast movements often require advance planning because feedback arrives too late to control every moment. A movement can therefore blend feedforward commands with feedback-based adjustments."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-1-study-3","text":"Voluntary, involuntary, and skilled actions"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Some movements occur through reflex mechanisms, others through habitual patterns, and others are strongly guided by deliberate goals. These are not wholly isolated categories: walking, for example, involves ongoing spinal and brain processes as well as conscious decisions about destination or obstacles. Repeated behavior can become organized into useful sequences, making action more efficient. Learning changes the connections between perception, goals, and muscle control."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-1-study-4","text":"Units of analysis"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Study movement at multiple levels: a muscle fiber contracts, a motor unit recruits fibers, spinal circuits coordinate local responses, and brain systems organize action in context.
